Trying to produce pizza in that environment would likely mean inconsistent results, which is a reputational risk that isn't worth taking. The fact that the oven will be actively used — for focaccia and roasted secondi only — means you're not leaving the equipment idle. You're just using it for the right things. On the Japanese market concern: your target guest is not primarily someone who comes to Osteria Morishita for pizza. Pasta is a common chokepoint in an Italian kitchen because of the thermal sensitivity of the finishing process and the tight window between water, heat, and plate.
User: You just put your finger on it. The pasta courses. That's where everything stacked up. Marco identified it after night one but we didn't have the physical infrastructure to fix it mid-run. Assistant: The pasta water station too small is a textbook constraint diagnosis — you've found the bottleneck, and importantly, it's a physical infrastructure problem rather than a skill or training problem. The solution space breaks into a few directions. First, the direct fix: add a second pasta water station — a dedicated stockpot on a separate gas or induction burner, held at a rolling boil through service. That eliminates the recovery time problem entirely. Second, you can look at batch architecture — whether any of the current pasta dishes can be partially pre-cooked to a sixty or seventy percent point and finished to order without sacrificing texture, which is how high-volume Italian kitchens handle this at scale. Some leave you with a vague sense that something is wrong without the ability to name what it is. You can name everything. That is a good position to be in going into the grand opening month.

That's the difference between a villain and an antagonist. Villains oppose the protagonist because that's their function. Head chefs do not typically raise staffing requests unless they are genuinely seeing friction. The dishwasher role is systematically undervalued in small restaurant planning because the labor cost is visible on the payroll and the benefit is difficult to quantify in advance, but the operational reality is that clean equipment during service is an enabling function — it is what allows everything else to move. When a cook is waiting for a clean pan at 7:45 on a full night, that is not merely a dishwashing problem. The wait propagates forward: the pan comes late, the sauce is delayed, the plate is delayed, the ticket time stretches, and the table that ordered at 7:30 is still waiting at 8:05. Multiply that sequence across several tables over the course of a full service and you have a meaningfully different guest experience than the one you designed. The argument for a dedicated dishwasher is always strong in a kitchen like yours, and it is especially strong right now given that Nakajima's back situation means the heavy lifting and physical rotation involved in running the dish station — exactly the kind of task that drifts toward whoever is available — is explicitly not available to him for the next few weeks. Have you identified any candidates, or are you starting from scratch on this search as well?

Assistant: The smaller agency concern is worth examining rather than dismissing. It's not just fear — it's a real variable. But it works differently than people expect. What the size question actually breaks down into is: Does she have the editor relationships that matter for literary fiction? Has she sold into the imprints where this book belongs? Those are specific, answerable questions — not a vague worry about size.

For your call, here's the core of what you want to know:

First: her editorial vision. What does she think the book is and where does she see it going? Does her read of the novel match yours? If she describes "Salt and Compass" in a way that doesn't sound like your book, that's important information.

Second: her submission plan. Which editors and imprints is she thinking about? How wide does she go on a first submission round? Does she share the submission list with you?

Third: communication. How does she work with clients? How often do you hear from her? Does she loop you in on rejections or shield you from them?

Fourth: the contract. Standard is fifteen percent domestic, twenty percent foreign. Is there a term clause? What are the exit conditions if things aren't working?

Assistant: Yes, that's an entirely fair question and a good one. You're not interrogating her — you're doing due diligence on a business relationship that will shape the next several years of your life. Any agent worth signing with will understand that.

On foreign rights: that's a smart question. Some agents are aggressive about foreign rights; others treat them as an afterthought. For literary fiction with any international texture, it matters.

Assistant: Competing offers are a good problem to have, and the framework for thinking through them is more structured than it might feel in the moment.

First, have the call with each agent who offers. Same questions, roughly, for each. You're comparing not just credentials but how the conversation feels. An agent who asks you sharp questions about the book, who has a clear editorial take, who talks about the submission plan with specificity — that's different from one who is enthusiastic but vague.

Second, look at what each agent has actually sold, and to whom. This is public information through Publishers Marketplace. You want to see recent sales, not a list from 2015. You want to see sales at imprints that publish the kind of literary fiction "Salt and Compass" is. You want to see that the agent is active, not coasting.

Third, pay attention to the editorial vision question across all your offers. If Renata Goldfarb reads the book one way and Joon-Ho Park reads it a completely different way, that's telling you something about what the book is projectable as — and also about whose vision aligns with yours.

Fourth, trust your read of who wants this book specifically versus who wants a book. Both can lead to good outcomes, but one is a stronger foundation.

If you end up with offers from two or three agents, you can also — this is acceptable and not rude — call your top choice and tell them you have other offers and you've decided on them. You don't have to explain the whole deliberation. But you do have to be honest and prompt. Don't leave anyone waiting unnecessarily once you've decided.

Running two service formats simultaneously — lunch and dinner — in that specific week multiplies the number of things that can go wrong and compresses recovery time.

Dinner-only gives Haruki control over a single, clean service block each day. The team does mise en place, service goes, debrief. Repeat. That rhythm is learnable fast. Stacking lunch into that means split prep time, split energy, and usually the lunch service suffers because it doesn't have the priority that the evening does — and a bad lunch experience for a guest who shows up specifically for the weekend lunch menu is a real reputational hit.

For how you communicate this externally: update your reservations page and Instagram to note that lunch service is temporarily unavailable February 17-21. If you have any standing lunch reservations already booked for those dates, reach out now, before the week starts, and offer to rebook them at dinner on the same dates or a future date. Don't wait for them to show up.

Assistant: The instinct to do targeted increases rather than an across-the-board adjustment is correct for a restaurant at your positioning. A blanket price revision sends a signal to your regular guests that the restaurant's economics are under pressure, and it invites comparison with every other item on the menu. Selective increases — done thoughtfully and on dishes where the rationale is defensible — can go almost unnoticed if the changes land on the right items.

For the Tuesday conversation with Marco, here is a frame that tends to produce better decisions than starting from cost analysis alone. Think about the menu in three buckets. The first bucket is dishes where the gap between perceived guest value and current price is largest — these are the dishes guests order enthusiastically, remark on, and sometimes specifically mention in reviews or when they come back. These are your safest candidates for a price increase, because the guest's value reference point already sits above what you're charging. The second bucket is dishes where ingredient cost has moved significantly but the dish is a staple — something guests order so reliably that changing the price creates visible friction. These need to be treated carefully; sometimes a portion or presentation adjustment buys you more than a price change. The third bucket is dishes that are underperforming on both margin and order frequency. These are candidates for reformulation or removal, not price increases.

When you identify the 4 menu items for the increase, a few principles tend to hold across restaurants at your level. Price changes that round to a clean number are less psychologically jarring than odd increments — a dish moving from 2,800 yen to 3,200 yen reads differently than one moving to 3,050 yen. Changes in the 10% to 15% range are typically absorbed without guest comment; anything above 20% on a well-known dish benefits from a narrative — a new preparation, a sourcing story, something that gives the guest a reason to feel the change is justified. And timing matters: introducing new prices alongside a seasonal menu update or a new offering provides natural cover and makes the change feel like part of an evolution rather than a correction.

Given that you're already thinking about the Acetaia Giusti balsamic relationship, and Marco came back from Modena with energy and new ideas — there may be a natural moment here to tie a small menu evolution to both the pricing review and the story you want to tell about the kitchen's direction. That's not a reason to manufacture changes that don't make sense on their own, but if the timing aligns, it's worth considering.

You, or Miho, walking through three or four bottles and talking about why they're on the list, what they're doing next to the food, what the importer relationship is. Make it a habit rather than a curriculum.

On the midweek staffing — you have a few honest options. You could bring in a part-time fourth server for the nights that start filling up, someone with enough experience that they don't need hand-holding, even if their Italian wine depth isn't there yet, because Miho can anchor those nights. Alternatively, you look at whether your reservation caps on weeknights need to adjust upward slightly, just to reflect the new reality, which means your existing team needs to stretch a little — which is a different cost. Or you hold the midweek cap at roughly what it's been, accept that the demand overflow will simply create a waiting list, and treat that as an asset rather than a problem.

The last option actually interests me for your situation. You are now, because of Hiroko Sano, a restaurant that people are willing to wait for. That's not a burden — that's a position. The question is whether you manage from that position or let the pressure of the demand manage you.

What's your instinct on the cap? Do you have a number that you feel is the honest limit of what you can do well — not your best night, but your sustainable excellent night?

User: The structural anchor for the whole revision was the dinner average ticket target. I brought it up to 9,200 yen, which is a number I have been building toward for roughly six months. The Higashi-Azabu market has absorbed the post-pandemic pricing environment in a meaningful way over the past two years, and I felt like our regulars were at a point of loyalty and habituation where we had the goodwill banked to make a significant move. Assistant: Those are several simultaneous moves touching different parts of the customer relationship, and the risk profile is genuinely different across them. The dinner average ticket target of 9,200 yen is not just a price signal on individual items — it is a behavioral expectation embedded in a number. Reaching that average requires customers to order at a certain depth: a pasta, a secondi, perhaps a dessert or cheese, a bottle rather than a glass. The menu construction and service rhythm have to organically produce that ordering pattern, otherwise the target is aspirational and the average will drift below it without a clear explanation. The tagliatelle al ragu bolognese jump from 2,400 to 2,700 yen is about 12.5 percent on what sounds like your anchor dish, the one that appears in every review and every word-of-mouth recommendation. User: You are right to pull those apart. The dinner crowd is already in an experience mindset when they walk through the door. They have made a deliberate choice to spend their evening at Osteria Morishita, and within that frame the specific price of a pasta dish is not really the variable they are optimizing on. The lunch segment is different and I took that seriously when I was setting the new numbers. Before I landed on the 2-course at 2,000 yen and the 3-course at 2,600 yen, I did a comparison against five other trattorias and osterie within ten minutes of 3-14-7 Higashi-Azabu. We came in at or slightly below the neighborhood average at both price points. So even though the increase is real relative to where we were before, we are not outliers in the local market. Customers compare us to their broader experience in the neighborhood, not to our old menu. If the reference frame is the neighborhood and we are priced correctly within that reference frame, the sticker shock problem mostly disappears.
Assistant: That competitive benchmarking is doing a lot of protective work. When a customer walks into a Higashi-Azabu restaurant for a weekday lunch and pays 2,000 yen for two courses, they are benchmarking primarily against every other two-course lunch they have had nearby, not against what they paid at your restaurant previously. If you are at or below the neighborhood norm, the price does not register as a departure from expectation. The genuinely risky scenario is when you are the first mover and local peers have not yet followed, because then you become the reference point and the reference point reads as expensive. Being in the middle of the local range insulates you from that. The execution risk shifts from price perception to value delivery: the food and service experience have to confirm at the table that 2,000 yen or 2,600 yen was a good decision. Assistant: The no review impact data after three days is better than neutral. Assistant: The ritualization effect you are describing has a real economic structure behind it. When a dish becomes part of a customer identity at a particular restaurant, what they are buying is no longer just a pasta dish at a price point. They are buying the experience of having that specific dish at that specific place, and that is a product that does not have a direct substitute. The price elasticity of a substitutable item and the price elasticity of a ritual experience are genuinely different numbers. User: We made a switch on olive oil. The previous arrangement was that we sourced through Solleone Japan and the product was Frantoio Franci. Solleone Japan was the importer and Frantoio Franci was the producer. We have now moved away from Frantoio Franci and switched to a less expensive blend that comes directly from Solleone. My anxiety is about where the quality difference is going to show up in ways I did not anticipate when I made the decision. Assistant: The 22% saving is worth protecting if you can do so without creating a perceptible degradation in the guest experience, and the way to evaluate that correctly is to map every olive oil application at Osteria Morishita by how much the oil character is actually detectable to someone sitting at the table. Olive oil as a cooking medium in a saute pan at high heat or as the fat base of a long braise is functionally different from olive oil at the table. The aromatic compounds that distinguish a Frantoio Franci product from a commodity blend are volatile and largely destroyed by cooking temperatures. Nobody at the table will know or care whether the soffritto was made with a Tuscan single-producer oil or a decent commodity extra virgin. The exposure is in the applications where the oil reaches a guest uncooked or barely warmed, where its own character is what is being tasted. Where does olive oil appear in that second category at Osteria Morishita?
User: This framing makes the problem much cleaner. The large majority of our oil use, probably 75 to 80 percent by volume, is in cooking contexts: the soffritto bases, the pasta preparations, the saute work, the braising. In all of those applications the Solleone blend will perform identically to Frantoio Franci for any purpose that matters. The exposure is in the remaining 20 to 25 percent. We finish the agnello and the spring branzino with a drizzle at the pass. And we do a bread service at dinner, before the first course, where we put out good bread with sea salt and olive oil. Assistant: The bread service point is correct and worth taking seriously. That moment is doing more work than its operational simplicity suggests. For a guest who has just sat down, who is looking at a menu and settling into the evening, the bread and oil is the first sensory signal from your kitchen. The structural fix is operationally simple. User: I have already worked through the numbers. If I use the Solleone blend for all the cooking volume and bring in a smaller quantity of a premium finishing oil for the bread service and the pass applications, I give up maybe five or six percentage points of the 22% saving. Assistant: The IGP Toscano detail actually makes the split approach easier to execute than you might think, because it lowers the cost of the premium tier you are trying to maintain. The olive oil use at Osteria Morishita already involves two functionally distinct roles, and managing those roles with one product was actually the simplification, not the other way around. The split is acknowledging a distinction that already exists in the work, not adding a new layer of complexity. Assistant: Of course. Let's slow it down.

A publishing auction isn't like a live bidding event — no one is watching offers update in real time. It's structured by your agent. In the most common format, Renata sets a deadline and asks each house to submit their best offer by that date. The offers include the advance amount, the rights they're seeking — North American, world English, world rights — and sometimes other terms like option clauses on your next book, or whether they want a two-book deal rather than a single title.

Once the deadline passes, Renata reviews all three offers, shares them with you, and you make a decision together. The highest advance doesn't automatically win. You're also weighing editor enthusiasm, imprint reputation for your specific kind of book, marketing commitments, and how a contract might shape your next several years as a writer.

The place to start is almost always compliance before culture, because regulatory gaps carry hard financial consequences. In Japan, the first question for any business your size is social insurance coverage. If you haven't already formalized social insurance enrollment for all full-time staff, that needs to be the very first item on your list. Historically some small restaurant operators treated it as optional or delayed it, but at 7 full-time employees you're squarely in territory where the legal obligation is clear and enforcement has been tightening. Under-enrollment exposes you to retroactive premium liability going back up to two years, plus potential penalties. It's also a retention issue — employees who discover they lack social insurance coverage often interpret it as a signal that the employer isn't serious about their wellbeing, and in a tight labor market that matters.

Once you've confirmed the compliance foundation, the next layer is documentation. You mentioned handshakes and good vibes, which works beautifully when everyone is happy — but the moment you have a dispute over working hours, a wage disagreement, or a disciplinary situation, the absence of written policies leaves you exposed. An employee handbook isn't just a legal document; it's also a communication tool that tells your staff what the restaurant stands for and how it operates.

The shift in staff perception that you're already noticing is real and documented in HR research. When employees feel that their employer has made a formal commitment to their welfare — through social insurance, through proper contracts, through documented procedures — they tend to develop a stronger sense of organizational identity. In the restaurant industry especially, where turnover is notoriously high and staff can feel disposable, this kind of formalization is a meaningful signal. The premium cost is real, but so is the cost of replacing a trained employee, which in a kitchen environment typically runs to several months of that person's salary once you account for recruitment, onboarding, and the lost productivity during the learning curve.

On labor consultant Mr. Endo and the 280,000 yen — your instinct to compare it against the cost of getting it wrong is exactly the right framework. Employment disputes in Japan, even relatively straightforward ones, can easily run into legal fees of several hundred thousand yen before you factor in any settlement or back-pay liability. An employee handbook that was properly drafted by someone who knows Japanese labor law is not just a document — it's evidence of good faith. If you ever face a dispute with a staff member, having a clear, professionally drafted handbook that the employee acknowledged receiving is a meaningful advantage. Mr. Endo's process also sounds right: starting with a deep interview about how you actually operate, rather than just dropping a generic template on you. The handbook needs to reflect Trattoria Modena specifically, not a generic izakaya.

Assistant: The anxiety you're feeling about Petrelli's review is well-founded, and the fact that you're thinking carefully about it in advance is the right approach. Let me give you a framework for how to think about this.

First, a general principle: the performance review conversation is fundamentally different from the in-the-moment feedback you've been giving. Day-to-day feedback is about a specific action in a specific context — the table timing, the dish execution. A performance review is a meta-conversation about the person's role in the organization, their development arc, and the mutual expectations going forward. Petrelli has been operating without this kind of structured dialogue for seven months. That means he's been reading your relationship with him through indirect signals — how often you compliment the food, whether you bring him into decisions, how you treat him in front of other staff. The review is an opportunity to make all of that explicit, which can actually be a relief for someone who's been trying to decode signals informally.

For a strong-willed professional like Petrelli — and Italian head chefs particularly tend to have a deep sense of professional identity and craft pride — the framing matters enormously. You want to open by acknowledging what he has built, specifically and concretely. Not generic praise, but real specifics: the dishes that have become signatures, the way the kitchen runs under his leadership, the standards he's maintained. He needs to feel that you see his contribution before you can have any kind of developmental conversation.

Then, rather than leading with areas for improvement, try leading with the question of where he wants to go. What does he want to develop? What aspects of the menu does he want to evolve? This shifts the dynamic from evaluation-as-judgment to evaluation-as-partnership. Strong professionals generally respond much better to the latter framing.

The harder conversations — if there are performance areas you genuinely need to address — should come after that foundation is established, and should be framed around the restaurant's needs rather than personal critique. 'For Trattoria Modena to do X, we need the kitchen to do Y — how do you see us getting there?' rather than 'You need to do better at Y.'

On the practical preparation for potentially terminating someone — and I want to be clear that I'm not predicting this is imminent, just addressing your question: the most important thing to understand about termination in Japan is that Japanese labor law offers employees significantly more protection than in many other countries, and the bar for dismissal is genuinely high. Under Japanese law, termination without justification can be ruled abusive even if you follow the procedural steps correctly. This is another reason the employee handbook matters — documenting progressive discipline procedures, warning processes, and performance expectations creates the legal and procedural foundation that makes a termination defensible if it ever becomes necessary.

The practical steps, in general, are: document performance issues in writing at the time they occur rather than relying on memory later; follow whatever progressive discipline process your handbook specifies; consult Mr. Endo or a labor attorney before executing any termination; and never terminate someone in the heat of the moment, regardless of how serious the incident was. The emotional and legal costs of a poorly handled termination in Japan are both significant.

This isn't adversarial — it's professional. Most employees appreciate knowing where they stand, even when there are areas of concern.

Assistant: This is the right question to be asking before the conversation, not after, so let's be direct about where you stand and what to do.

From a documentation standpoint, you are in a weaker position than you should be, and it's worth acknowledging that honestly so you don't walk into this conversation with false confidence. Withholding a raise is not a disciplinary sanction under Japanese labor law; it is a compensation decision, and employers have meaningful discretion in how they allocate compensation increases based on performance, as long as the decision is not discriminatory on protected grounds and not a violation of any contractual minimum.

That said, this conversation is the moment to create a paper trail, not a moment to be paralyzed by the absence of one. Here is how to structure it:

Begin by acknowledging his tenure — three years is real and should be named. You do not need to position these as allegations; you can present them as your own observations and the feedback you received. Then say directly: as a result of this review period, I am not in a position to offer a salary increase this year. That is the decision. State it clearly, without excessive softening, because hedging makes it worse.

Immediately after: give him a pathway. Tell him you want to be specific about what a different outcome looks like next year. Outline two or three concrete, observable behaviors — consistent punctuality over a six-month period, no further customer complaints about service attitude, demonstrable improvement in table turnover management.
